# Adversarial Attacks Against LLM Routers

An adversarial attack against LLM routers by using a paraphraser Seq2Seq model. 
The goal is to rewrite a query so it remains semantically equivalent, while increasing the probability that a router
sends it to a stronger and more expensive model, thus focusing on cost-escalation attacks. 

[GitHub repository](https://github.com/renonesopocaj/adversarial-paraphraser)

<h2 class='titleHead'>Adversarial Paraphrasing for Cost-Escalation Attacks against

 <div class='author'><span class='ptmr8t-x-x-120'>Caviglia Matteo | 414369, Mamouri Mohamed | 362231,</span>
<br />   <span class='ptmr8t-x-x-120'>Pellonara Mauro | 416280, Senoner Jacopo | 416355,</span>

   <section class='abstract' role='doc-abstract'>
<h3 class='abstracttitle'>
<span class='ptmb8t-'>Abstract</span>
</h3>
     <!-- l. 91 --><p class='noindent'><span class='ptmr8t-'>LLM routers send user queries to different language models to optimise performance, and are
     deployed in OpenRouter, Unify, and OpenAI’s GPT-5 auto-router. Recent work shows these
     routers are adversarially fragile, but the attacks are mainly based on appending special suffixes</span>
     <!-- l. 92 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>s</mi></math></span>
     <span class='ptmr8t-'>to a query </span><!-- l. 92 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span><span class='ptmr8t-'>.
     We propose a Seq2Seq adversarial </span><span class='ptmri8t-'>paraphraser </span><span class='ptmr8t-'>that fully rewrites a query </span><!-- l. 93 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span>
     <span class='ptmr8t-'>into a semantically equivalent variant </span><!-- l. 93 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msup><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle class='text'><mtext>adv</mtext></mstyle></mrow></msup></math></span><span class='ptmr8t-'>,
     affecting  the  router’s  decision.  Our  main  findings  are:  (i)  we  introduce  a  novel  black-box
     cost-escalation attack against LLM routers based on adversarial paraphrasing; (ii) with loosened
     similarity constraints, an aggressive attacker compromises all three routers in our training pool
     (ASR </span><!-- l. 93 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>≥</mo></mrow></math></span>
     <span class='ptmr8t-'>15% on each) at moderate semantic similarity (</span><!-- l. 93 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.91</mn></mrow></math></span><span class='ptmr8t-'>);
     (iii) under stricter similarity constraints (similarity </span><!-- l. 93 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.94</mn></mrow></math></span><span class='ptmr8t-'>),
     a conservative attacker still successfully attacks </span><!-- l. 93 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mn>2</mn></math></span>
     <span class='ptmr8t-'>out of </span><!-- l. 93 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mn>3</mn></math></span>
     <span class='ptmr8t-'>routers in the pool; (iv) attacks generalize across out-of-distribution data but transfer poorly to
     unseen routers.</span>
</p>
</section>
   <h3 class='sectionHead' id='introduction'><span class='titlemark'>1    </span> <a id='x1-10001'></a>Introduction</h3>
<!-- l. 98 --><p class='noindent'>Large Language Model (LLM) routers control inference cost and output quality by directing queries
towards the most appropriate LLM, optimizing performance, latency, and cost.
</p><!-- l. 100 --><p class='indent'>   Routers are vulnerable to adversarial attacks [<a href='#Xzhang2026rerouteguard'>21</a>]; Zhang et al. [<a href='#Xzhang2026rerouteguard'>21</a>] identifies three threat scenarios:
cost escalation, quality hijacking, safety bypass. Cost-escalation attacks are the focus of this project; their
aim is to maliciously manipulate routers by tricking them into sending simple queries to expensive
models.
</p><!-- l. 102 --><p class='indent'>   Existing attack methods work via token-level perturbations (sometimes called <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>confounder gadgets</span>)
appended at the end of the sentence, and typically degrade grammar and alter semantics [<a href='#Xtang2026routeromeattackdirecting'>15</a>]. We propose
an <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>adversarial paraphraser </span>able to execute cost-escalation attacks by rewording a given query into an
adversarial one, while maintaining semantics. We motivate the use of a paraphraser to reflect realistic
threats, where an attacker rephrases their query to sound more complex, triggering routing to a more
capable model.
</p><!-- l. 105 --><p class='indent'>   Furthermore, our thesis is that adversarial paraphrasing offers a stealthier threat model than simple
token substitution or appending when used against LLM routers. Li et al. [<a href='#Xli2020bertattack'>7</a>] and Jin et al. [<a href='#Xjin2020textfooler'>3</a>] present
adversarial attacks able to preserve semantics and successfully attack BERT classifiers. In sentiment
analysis, for instance, Iyyer et al. [<a href='#Xiyyer-etal-2018-adversarial'>2</a>] demonstrated that adversarially paraphrased examples can
reliably breaking classifiers. More recently, Kaneko [<a href='#Xkaneko2026paraphrasingadversarialattackllmasareviewer'>5</a>] explored paraphrasing attacks against
LLM-as-a-reviewer systems, showing how simple rewording can skew automated evaluation
scores.
</p><!-- l. 107 --><p class='indent'>   <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>Paraphrasing attacks remain unexplored against LLM routers, establishing the critical gap our project
addresses.</span>
</p><!-- l. 109 --><p class='indent'>   Our main findings are that our paraphraser is indeed able to attack the <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT </span>and <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan </span>training routers
with an ASR above <!-- l. 109 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>70</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>,
but falls well short of this mark against <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal</span>. Moreover, we find the semantic
preservation terms are necessary to preserve meaning, and semantic drift is accentuated if
excessive length of the paraphrases is not penalized. Finally, although semantic preservation
prevents our paraphraser from overfitting to a specific router, it alone does not seem to guarantee
transferability.
</p>
   <h3 class='sectionHead' id='methodology'><span class='titlemark'>2    </span> <a id='x1-20002'></a>Methodology</h3>
<!-- l. 118 --><p class='noindent'>Our framework for creating the adversarial attacks consists in training a <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>paraphraser model</span>
<!-- l. 118 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>G</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>𝜃</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> using Reinforcement
Learning (RL). In practice, we fine-tune T5-chatGPT-paraphraser [<a href='#Xchatgpt_paraphraser'>16</a>], a 0.22B-parameter Seq2Seq model, so that, given a
query <!-- l. 118 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span> routed to a low-cost
model <!-- l. 118 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='script'><mi>M</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mstyle class='text'><mtext>weak</mtext></mstyle></mrow></msub></math></span>, the generated
<!-- l. 118 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msup><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup></math></span> is routed towards a
high-cost model <!-- l. 118 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='script'><mi>M</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mstyle class='text'><mtext>strong</mtext></mstyle></mrow></msub></math></span>.
The model choice is motivated by recent evidence that small paraphrasers, such as the chosen model, can
be competitive with much larger instruction-tuned models for paraphrase generation [<a href='#Xlemesle2026fewshot'>6</a>]. Since our attack
only requires meaning-preserving rewrites rather than open-ended generations, a lightweight Seq2Seq
paraphraser offers a better fit for the cost-escalation threat model.
</p>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='grpo-training'><span class='titlemark'>2.1    </span> <a id='x1-30002.1'></a>GRPO Training</h4>
<!-- l. 122 --><p class='noindent'>We implement a custom GRPO [<a href='#Xdeepseek-math'>22</a>] training pipeline, as existing RL libraries lack support for
encoder-decoder models or are not maintained.
</p><!-- l. 124 --><p class='indent'>   Let <!-- l. 124 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>G</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>𝜃</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> be the trainable
paraphraser and <!-- l. 124 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>G</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>ref</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub></math></span>
a frozen copy of the initial paraphraser. For each clean query
<!-- l. 124 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>, we sample
<!-- l. 124 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>N</mi></math></span> completions, score them with the
composite reward <!-- l. 124 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant='italic'>𝑖𝑗</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>, and normalize
rewards (with their mean <!-- l. 124 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>μ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>
and standard deviation <!-- l. 124 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>σ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>)
within the group: <!-- l. 125 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant='italic'>𝑖𝑗</mi></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant='italic'>𝑖𝑗</mi></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo><msub><mrow><mi>μ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow>
  <mrow><msub><mrow><mi>σ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>+</mo><mi>𝜖</mi></mrow></mfrac>  <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo></mrow></math></span> For
each generated token <!-- l. 129 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>t</mi></math></span>,
we store the rollout log-probability, recompute the current policy log-probability, use the clipped PPO
surrogate, and include the usual KL penality, anchoring the policy to the frozen baseline. Details can be
found in Appendix <a href='#grpo'>A.3<!-- tex4ht:ref: subsec:appendixggrpo  --></a>.
</p>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='reward'><span class='titlemark'>2.2    </span> <a id='x1-40002.2'></a>Reward</h4>
<!-- l. 133 --><p class='noindent'>For a clean query <!-- l. 133 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span> and
generated paraphrase <!-- l. 133 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msup><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup></math></span>,
the reward uses three components: (i) a <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>routing term </span>that encourages cost escalation attacks, (ii) a
<span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>semantic-preservation term </span>that penalizes queries that drift from the meaning of the original query
<!-- l. 133 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span>,
and (iii) a <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>length-deviation penalty </span>that penalizes excessive length compared to
<!-- l. 133 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span>. We denote
<!-- l. 133 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>σ</mi></math></span> as the sigmoid function,
parameterized by a temperature <!-- l. 133 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>T</mi></math></span>.
The code supports two variants: an <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>additive </span>reward and a <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>multiplicative </span>reward.
</p>
<!-- l. 135 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='routing-term'><a id='x1-5000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Routing term.</span></span>
   For each router <!-- l. 137 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>k</mi></math></span> in
the active router pool <!-- l. 137 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='script'><mi>R</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>pool</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub></math></span>,
let <!-- l. 137 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo></mrow></math></span>
be the router confidence assigned to the strong model for input
<!-- l. 137 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>x</mi></math></span>. The routing term is
defined as the difference <!-- l. 138 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant='normal'>Δ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>,
between the confidence score of the query before and after being paraphrased, plus an optional weighted <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>flip bonus</span>
<!-- l. 138 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>f</mi></math></span> if the paraphrased query’s
score is now above <!-- l. 138 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></math></span>.
In formulas, given <!-- l. 139 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant='normal'>Δ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>clip</mi></mstyle><mspace class='negthinspace' width='-0.17em'></mspace> <mrow><mo fence='true' form='prefix'>(</mo><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mi>q</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo><mn>1</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo fence='true' form='postfix'>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>,
<!-- l. 141 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mi>f</mi> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <mi>𝕀</mi> <mrow><mo fence='true' form='prefix'>[</mo><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mi>q</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>&lt;</mo> <mn>0.5</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>≥</mo> <mn>0.5</mn></mrow><mo fence='true' form='postfix'>]</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. We
define <!--l. 144--><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>route</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo><msub><mrow> <mi mathvariant='normal'>Δ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>+</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>flip</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><mi>f</mi></mrow></math></span>.
The pooled routing reward is the weighted mean across active routers.
</p>
<!-- l. 148 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='semantic-similarity-term'><a id='x1-6000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Semantic similarity term.</span></span>
<a id='x1-6000doc'></a>
   This term starts from a clamped cosine <!-- l. 150 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mover accent='true'><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mo accent='true'>~</mo></mover></math></span>
between L2-normalized embeddings <!-- l. 150 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mstyle mathvariant='bold'><mi>𝐞</mi></mstyle></math></span>,
<!-- l. 150 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msup><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='bold'><mi>𝐞</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup></math></span>:
<!-- l. 151 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mover accent='true'><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mo accent='true'>~</mo></mover> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo><mi class='qopname'> max</mi><mo> ⁡<!-- FUNCTION APPLICATION --> </mo><mspace class='negthinspace' width='-0.17em'></mspace> <mrow><mo fence='true' form='prefix'>(</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><msup><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='bold'><mi>𝐞</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mi class='MathClass-op'>⊤</mi><mo> ⁡<!-- FUNCTION APPLICATION --></mo></mrow></msup><msup><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='bold'><mi>𝐞</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup></mrow><mo fence='true' form='postfix'>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. If NLI gating
is disabled <!-- l. 151 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>sim</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <mover accent='true'><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mo accent='true'>~</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>,
else, we compute contradiction probabilities in both directions:
<!-- l. 151 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mi>g</mi> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow>
<mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi>c</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>fwd</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>+</mo> <msup><mrow><mi>c</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>bwd</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo></mrow></math></span> and define
<!-- l. 151 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>sim</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <mi>g</mi><mover accent='true'><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mo accent='true'>~</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>. For the additive reward, a hard
<span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>similarity floor </span>which assigns <!-- l. 151 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>sim</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>
if <!-- l. 151 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mover accent='true'><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mo accent='true'>~</mo></mover> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>&lt;</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>τ</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>sim</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> can
be applied.
</p>
<!-- l. 153 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='length-penalty'><a id='x1-7000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Length penalty.</span></span>
   The length term discourages degenerate paraphrases that become much
shorter or longer than the original prompt. With whitespace-token length
<!-- l. 154 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mi>ℓ</mi><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>⋅</mo><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo></mrow></math></span> and tolerance
<!-- l. 154 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>τ</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>len</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub></math></span>, the penalty
is: <!-- l. 154 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>len</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo><mi class='qopname'> max</mi><mo> ⁡<!-- FUNCTION APPLICATION --> </mo><mspace class='negthinspace' width='-0.17em'></mspace> <mrow><mo fence='true' form='prefix'>(</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo> <mfrac><mrow><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>|</mo><mi>ℓ</mi><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo><mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo><mi>ℓ</mi><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mi>q</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>|</mo></mrow>
 <mrow><mi class='qopname'> max</mi><mo> ⁡<!-- FUNCTION APPLICATION --> </mo><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><mi>ℓ</mi><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mi>q</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo></mrow></mfrac>   <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>τ</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>len</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub></mrow><mo fence='true' form='postfix'>)</mo></mrow> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo></mrow></math></span>
</p>
<!-- l. 156 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='reward-variants'><a id='x1-8000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Reward Variants.</span></span>
   In the <span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>additive </span>version of the reward function, routing and semantic preservation are treated as separate objectives.
Given <!-- l. 157 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>l</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>r</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>s</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> weights
for each term: <!-- l. 158 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mi>R</mi> <mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>:</mo><mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>route</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>route</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>+</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>sim</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>sim</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>len</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>len</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo></mrow></math></span>
</p><!-- l. 160 --><p class='indent'>   We also define a <span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>multiplicative </span>reward: <!-- l. 161 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mi>R</mi> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>route</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub> <mrow><mo fence='true' form='prefix'>(</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>NLI</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup><msup><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>core</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>+</mo> <mi>f</mi><msub><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>NLI</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub></mrow><mo fence='true' form='postfix'>)</mo></mrow> <mo class='MathClass-bin' stretchy='false'>−</mo> <msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>len</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>len</mi></mstyle></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo></mrow></math></span>
The core term <!-- l. 162 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msup><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>core</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup></math></span>
modulates the base routing reward using semantic similarity. To penalize semantic drift, the NLI multiplier
<!-- l. 162 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>NLI</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup></math></span>
suppresses positive rewards and amplifies negative ones when a contradiction is detected between
<!-- l. 162 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span> and
<!-- l. 162 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msup><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup></math></span>.
Complete definitions for the core reward, NLI gates, and similarity thresholds are provided in
Appendix <a href='#implementation-details'>A.2<!-- tex4ht:ref: subsubsec:nlisemanticmodel  --></a>.
</p>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='training-methodologies'><span class='titlemark'>2.3    </span> <a id='x1-90002.3'></a>Training methodologies</h4>
<!-- l. 168 --><p class='noindent'><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Curriculum Learning</span> [<a href='#Xbengio2009curriculum'>1</a>] is used over training queries based on the router margin. We first introduce
samples whose clean routing score is closest to the decision threshold between the weak and strong model.
These borderline samples are more likely to trigger a shift in routing decisions after small paraphrasing
changes, thereby providing an early less-noisy reward signal.
</p>
<!-- l. 170 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='including-multiple-routers'><a id='x1-10000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Including multiple routers.</span></span>
<a id='x1-10000doc'></a>
   Our goal is to train a paraphraser effective against multiple routers, let
<!-- l. 173 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='script'><mi>R</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant='italic'>𝑝𝑜𝑜𝑙</mi></mrow></msub> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>=</mo> <mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>{</mo><mi>A</mi><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><mi>B</mi><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><mi>C</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>}</mo></mrow></math></span> for this subsection
only. We try two approaches: (i) we include all routers in the routing term of the reward, by taking the mean of the
routing terms <!-- l. 173 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>A</mi></math></span>,
<!-- l. 173 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>B</mi></math></span>,
<!-- l. 173 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>C</mi></math></span> (mixed
training in Yang et al. [<a href='#Xyang2026whendomainsinteract'>19</a>]); (ii) we train on one router at a time (sequential training in Yang et al. [<a href='#Xyang2026whendomainsinteract'>19</a>]).
We order routers based on their vulnerability, measured by the maximum attack success rate
achieved during individual training runs against each router, and report the exact sequence in
Section <a href='#x1-15000doc'>3.2<!-- tex4ht:ref: subsec:trainingrregimes  --></a>.
</p><!-- l. 175 --><p class='indent'>   Rather than training to converge on <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>A</mi></math></span>,
then on <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>B</mi></math></span>, and finally
on <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>C</mi></math></span>, we use a “cyclic”
schedule: we train for <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>K</mi></math></span>
epochs on <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>A</mi></math></span>,
then <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>K</mi></math></span>
epochs on <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>B</mi></math></span>,
then <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>K</mi></math></span>
epochs on <!-- l. 175 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>C</mi></math></span>,
and repeat this cycle. A similar pattern, based on periodic revisiting of tasks over training, was explored by
Speckmann and Eimer [<a href='#Xspeckmann2025taskschedulingforgetting'>13</a>]. The cyclic schedule is designed to mitigate over-specialization to
the most recently trained router, a known issue in the analogous setting of multi-domain RL.
[<a href='#Xyang2026whendomainsinteract'>19</a>].
</p>
   <h3 class='sectionHead' id='experiments'><span class='titlemark'>3    </span> <a id='x1-110003'></a>Experiments</h3>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='data'><span class='titlemark'>3.1    </span> <a id='x1-120003.1'></a>Data</h4>
<!-- l. 182 --><p class='noindent'>We draw queries from three public English question-answering datasets used as realistic user-query proxies:
TriviaQA [<a href='#Xjoshi-etal-2017-triviaqa'>4</a>] (open-domain factual), CommonsenseQA [<a href='#Xtalmor-etal-2019-commonsenseqa'>14</a>] (commonsense reasoning), and SVAMP [<a href='#Xpatel-etal-2021-nlp'>11</a>]
(arithmetic word problems). We avoid datasets used to train the routers in our pool, so that the held-out routers
are evaluated on disjoint data and we do not overfit to the training routers. We keep the question text as the query
<!-- l. 182 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span> and remove duplicates,
yielding <!-- l. 182 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>≈</mo><mspace class='negthinspace' width='-0.17em'></mspace><mn>98</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>,</mo><mn>129</mn></mrow></math></span> unique queries;
for every query and router <!-- l. 182 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mi>k</mi> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>∈</mo><mstyle mathvariant='script'><mi>R</mi></mstyle></mrow></math></span>
we precompute and cache the routing decision. We use per-source stratified train/val/test splits, containing
only queries routed to the weak model, resulting in a reduced dataset (size and source mix in
Appendix <a href='#dataset-details'>A.1<!-- tex4ht:ref: appendix:data  --></a>).
</p>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='experimental-configuration'><span class='titlemark'>3.2    </span> <a id='x1-130003.2'></a>Experimental Configuration</h4>
<!-- l. 187 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='router-pool'><a id='x1-14000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Router Pool.</span></span>
   We integrate six routers: two RouteLLM [<a href='#Xong2025routellm'>10</a>] variants (<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT</span>, <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal</span>)
and <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan</span> [<a href='#Xlu2025routerarena'>8</a>] are used for training, while <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>R2</span> [<a href='#Xxue2026r2router'>17</a>] and other two RouteLLM [<a href='#Xong2025routellm'>10</a>] variants
(<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-MatrixFactorization</span>, <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-SimilarityWeighted</span>) are held out to measure
transferability to unseen routers. Details on all six routers are provided in Appendix <a href='#router-pool1'>A.4.1<!-- tex4ht:ref: appendix:routerppool  --></a>.
</p>
<!-- l. 190 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='training-regimes'><a id='x1-15000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Training Regimes.</span></span>
<a id='x1-15000doc'></a>
   Runs are either single-router or multi-router. Multi-router runs train against all three routers in sequence
(<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT</span><!-- l. 192 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>→</mo></mrow></math></span>
<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan</span><!-- l. 192 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>→</mo></mrow></math></span>
<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal</span>), spending two epochs on each before rotating. Hyperparameter and configuration
details are in Appendix <a href='#reproducibility'>A.4.2<!-- tex4ht:ref: appendix:configs  --></a>. All experiments were run on a single A100 GPU, 40 GB VRAM and used
AdamW as optimizer.
</p>
<!-- l. 194 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='training-efficiency'><a id='x1-16000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Training Efficiency.</span></span>
   In  our  first  implementation,  training  time  took
<!-- l. 194 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>∼</mo></mrow></math></span>5 hours per epoch with only
<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT </span>in <!-- l. 194 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mstyle mathvariant='script'><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle class='text'><mtext>𝓅oo𝓁</mtext></mstyle></mrow></msub></mstyle></math></span>. Batch
routing reduced this to <!-- l. 194 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow> <mo class='MathClass-rel' stretchy='false'>∼</mo></mrow></math></span>20
minutes per epoch, with parallel routing further cutting time when multiple routers are used.
</p>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='evaluation'><span class='titlemark'>3.3    </span> <a id='x1-170003.3'></a>Evaluation</h4>
<!-- l. 199 --><p class='noindent'>For a router <!-- l. 199 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>k</mi></math></span>, let
<!-- l. 199 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo></mrow></math></span> be the model
selected for an input <!-- l. 199 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>x</mi></math></span>,
while <!-- l. 199 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msubsup><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='script'><mi>M</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>weak</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mi>k</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo></mrow></msubsup></math></span>
and <!-- l. 199 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msubsup><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='script'><mi>M</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>strong</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mo class='MathClass-open' stretchy='false'>(</mo><mi>k</mi><mo class='MathClass-close' stretchy='false'>)</mo></mrow></msubsup></math></span>
represent the sets of weak and strong models, respectively.
</p><!-- l. 201 --><p class='indent'>   Our primary metrics are: (i) <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>Attack Success Rate (ASR)</span>: The proportion of queries routed to a weak
model that are successfully "flipped" to a strong model when paraphrased. A more detailed description is
provided in Appendix <a href='#attack-success-rate-formulation'>A.2.3<!-- tex4ht:ref: appendix:asr  --></a>.
</p><!-- l. 204 --><p class='indent'>   (ii) <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>Semantic Similarity</span>: The cosine similarity between the embeddings of
<!-- l. 204 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>q</mi></math></span> and its
paraphrase <!-- l. 204 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msup><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mrow><mstyle mathvariant='normal'><mi>adv</mi></mstyle></mrow></msup></math></span>,
computed via an embedding model (see <a href='#implementation-details'>A.2<!-- tex4ht:ref: subsubsec:nlisemanticmodel  --></a>).
</p><!-- l. 222 --><p class='indent'>   We also monitor two additional metrics: the KL divergence with respect to the original paraphraser
and the average generated sentence length.
</p>
   <h3 class='sectionHead' id='results-and-analysis'><span class='titlemark'>4    </span> <a id='x1-180004'></a>Results and Analysis</h3>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='single-router-attacks'><span class='titlemark'>4.1    </span> <a id='x1-190004.1'></a>Single Router Attacks</h4>
<!-- l. 229 --><p class='noindent'>We first validate the viability of single-router attacks by training separate paraphrasers against <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT</span>,
<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan</span>, and <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal</span>. As summarized in Table <a href='#checkpoint-configurations-for-the-four-singlerouter-runs-brt-chy-csl-cag-abbreviations-as-in-tableref-tabablationlegend-each-was-tuned-independently-against-its-target-router-the-dash-for-the-cag-step-size-denotes-a-constant-nonstepped-schedule'>5<!-- tex4ht:ref: tab:single_router_configs  --></a>, we observe significant variability in router
robustness. The target <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan </span>is the most vulnerable, as our single-router model (<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>CHY</span>) achieves
<!-- l. 230 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>77</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>18</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR with a strong
semantic similarity of <!-- l. 230 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.871</mn></mrow></math></span>.
Similarly, <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT </span>is also vulnerable, showing an ASR of
<!-- l. 230 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>9.48</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> with
<!-- l. 230 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.926</mn></mrow></math></span>
similarity. Conversely, <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal </span>demonstrates high robustness as the <span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>CSL </span>run only yields a
<!-- l. 231 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.96</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR
with <!-- l. 231 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.890</mn></mrow></math></span>
similarity.
</p><!-- l. 233 --><p class='indent'>   The ablation study on <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal </span>shows key interactions among the KL weight
<!-- l. 233 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mi>β</mi></math></span>, similarity floor, and
validation performance. Figure <a href='#klasr-pareto-frontier-of-the-routellmcausal-sweep-each-marker-is-one-configuration-from-tableref-tabcausalsweepconfig-placed-at-its-kl-divergence-at-peak-asr-x-log-scale-and-its-peak-validation-asr-y-the-marker-colour-encodes-the-similarity-floor-sim-filled-markers-and-the-dashed-line-trace-the-nondominated-frontier-faded-markers-are-dominated-and-the-star-is-the-aggressive-variant-cag-asr-grows-monotonically-with-the-allowed-kl-even-the-best-causal-configuration-only-reaches-asr-and-only-by-tolerating-large-drift-which-confirms-that-routellmcausal-is-the-most-robust-router-in-the-training-pool'>17<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:causalssweepppareto  --></a> shows the ASR–paraphrasing tradeoff (KL; sim-floor): the aggressive <span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>CAG </span>run reaches
<!-- l. 233 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>4.68</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR by allowing lower
semantic similarity (<!-- l. 233 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.865</mn></mrow></math></span>).
</p>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='multirouter-attacks-and-baselines'><span class='titlemark'>4.2    </span> <a id='x1-200004.2'></a>Multi-Router Attacks and Baselines</h4>
<!-- l. 238 --><p class='noindent'>Our Finetuned model (<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>FT</span>) manages to balance semantic integrity (Sim:
<!-- l. 238 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.942</mn></mrow></math></span>) while attacking successfully
<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan </span>(<!-- l. 238 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>53</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>69</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR) and <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT</span>
(<!-- l. 238 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>6.12</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR). <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal</span>
remains relatively intact (<!-- l. 238 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.11</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>
ASR) (Figure <a href='#x1-43001r1'>8a<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:RESEEVAL  --></a>).
</p><!-- l. 240 --><p class='indent'>   In the <span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>AGG </span>(Aggressive) baseline, where KL penalties are loosened and the
PPO clip widened, the results shift radically. With an accepted similarity drop to
<!-- l. 240 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.907</mn></mrow></math></span>,
the  attacker  succeeds  across  the  pool:  it  breaks  <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan</span>
(<!-- l. 240 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>100</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR), <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT</span>
(<!-- l. 240 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>73</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>59</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR) and finally penetrates
<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal </span>with a <!-- l. 240 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>15</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>73</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>
ASR. As plotted in Figures <a href='#pareto-frontier-of-attack-success-rate-asr-vs-mean-semantic-similarity-on-the-routellmbert-indistribution-router-each-point-is-one-trained-checkpoint-the-dashed-line-connects-the-nondominated-front-marker-shapes-denote-run-category-baseline-reward-ablation-singlerouter-pretrained-the-xaxis-is-broken-to-isolate-the-zerosimilarityweight-outlier-zwsa-s-from-the-main-cluster'>11<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:pareto_bert  --></a>, <a href='#pareto-frontier-of-asr-vs-mean-semantic-similarity-on-the-chayan-indistribution-router-chayan-uses-a-different-decision-boundary-than-bertbased-routers-so-frontier-membership-differs-noticeably-across-the-two-indistribution-plots'>12<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:pareto_chayan  --></a>, <a href='#pareto-frontier-of-asr-vs-mean-semantic-similarity-on-the-routellmcausal-indistribution-router-the-aggressive-singlerouter-checkpoint-agg-anchors-the-highasr-end-of-the-frontier-reaching-the-highest-attack-rate-among-all-runs'>13<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:pareto_causal  --></a>, this illustrates a Pareto frontier between semantic fidelity and
successful cost-escalation attacks on LLM-Routers.
</p><!-- l. 242 --><p class='indent'>   Finally, we present a qualitative analysis of some example of paraphrases in Appendix <a href='#paraphrases-qualitative-analysis'>A.5<!-- tex4ht:ref: appendix:qualitative  --></a>.
</p>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='transferability'><span class='titlemark'>4.3    </span> <a id='x1-210004.3'></a>Transferability</h4>
<!-- l. 247 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='outofdistribution-routers'><a id='x1-22000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Out-of-distribution Routers.</span></span>
   Zero-shot transferability of crafted queries is tested on the three unseen routers introduced
in Section <a href='#experimental-configuration'>3.2<!-- tex4ht:ref: subsec:experimentalconfig  --></a>. As shown in Figures <a href='#x1-43002r2'>8b<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:RESTTRANSF  --></a>, <a href='#zeroshot-transfer-pareto-frontier-on-the-heldout-r-router-interestingly-the-zwsa-run-which-was-left-free-to-shape-its-paraphrases-without-constraints-does-not-transfer-well-suggesting-that-it-overfitted-to-the-training-routers-overall-since-no-checkpoint-was-trained-against-r-the-frontier-collapses-to-asr-indicating-limited-transferability-to-this-router'>14<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:pareto_r2  --></a>, <a href='#zeroshot-transfer-pareto-frontier-on-the-heldout-routellmmf-matrix-factorization-router-interestingly-the-unconstrained-zwsa-run-again-transfers-poorly-suggesting-that-freely-optimized-paraphrases-overfitted-to-the-training-routers-rather-than-learning-broadly-transferable-adversarial-patterns-as-with-r-no-checkpoint-was-trained-on-this-router-the-frontier-reflects-the-degree-to-which-adversarial-paraphrases-generalise-across-router-families'>15<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:pareto_mf  --></a>, <a href='#zeroshot-transfer-pareto-frontier-on-the-heldout-routellmsw-similarity-weighted-router-asr-drops-to-for-all-checkpoints-similarityweighted-routing-is-robust-to-the-paraphrasing-strategy-learned-in-training'>16<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:pareto_sw  --></a>, cross-router cost-escalation via
paraphrasing exhibits low transferability. Our <span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>FT </span>model records a maximum ASR of
<!-- l. 248 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>2.16</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> against <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>R2</span>, recording no damage
to <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>MF </span>or <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>SW </span>(<!-- l. 248 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.0</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR). Even the
<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>AGG </span>configuration peaks only at <!-- l. 248 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>2.70</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>
ASR on <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>R2</span>. This indicates that query-routing heuristics learn distinctly different latent boundaries, making
generalized black-box attacks via natural paraphrasing very difficult.
</p>
<!-- l. 250 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='outofdistribution-queries'><a id='x1-23000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Out-of-distribution queries.</span></span>
   Evaluating the final models on unseen data from IFEval (Figure <a href='#outofdistribution-evaluation-on-ifeval-test-asr-of-the-two-final-models-ft-agg-against-all-six-routers-on-heldout-instructionfollowing-prompts-that-are-new-to-both-the-paraphraser-and-the-routers-the-legend-reports-each-models-mean-semantic-similarity-the-cosine-similarity-between-the-stellaenbv-embeddings-of-the-original-query-q-and-its-paraphrase-q-adv-averaged-over-the-prompts-sectionref-subsecevaluation-the-attack-generalizes-offdistribution-on-the-training-routers-chayan-for-ftagg-routellmbert-routellmcausal-reproducing-the-indistribution-ranking-at-comparable-similarity-while-the-heldout-routers-stay-essentially-unattacked-mf-sw-at-r-generalization-is-therefore-to-new-data-not-new-routers'>10<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:oodiifeval  --></a>) shows that the attack generalizes
to unseen <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>data </span>but not unseen <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>routers</span>: ASR on the training routers stays high (<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>AGG</span>: <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan</span>
<!-- l. 251 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>88</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>6</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>, <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT</span>
<!-- l. 251 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>67</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>) at
similarity comparable to in-distribution, while the held-out <span class='ptmri8t-x-x-109'>routers </span>remain unattacked.
</p>
   <h4 class='subsectionHead' id='ablations'><span class='titlemark'>4.4    </span> <a id='x1-240004.4'></a>Ablations</h4>
<!-- l. 258 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='similarity-constraints'><a id='x1-25000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Similarity Constraints.</span></span>
   Controlling the similarity floor strictly influences attack possibilities. By removing semantic
penalties completely (<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>ZWSA</span>), the model degenerates into pure adversarial perturbations (Sim:
<!-- l. 258 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.268</mn></mrow></math></span>) but massively exploits
<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>Chayan </span>(<!-- l. 258 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>99</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>33</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> ASR) and
<span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-BERT </span>(<!-- l. 258 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>98</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>44</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>
ASR). Conversely, a very high similarity floor (<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>HSF</span>) preserves
<!-- l. 258 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.986</mn></mrow></math></span> Sim but
vastly dampens the attack potential compared to the baseline.
</p>
<!-- l. 263 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='curriculum-learning'><a id='x1-26000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Curriculum Learning.</span></span>
   Figure <a href='#routingreward-trajectories-rewardroutemean-for-the-baseline-finetuned-run-and-the-nocurriculum-ablation-over-training-steps-ft-a-shows-clean-periodic-spikes-aligned-with-the-cyclic-router-schedule-and-returns-to-a-stable-baseline-between-phases-whereas-nc-b-is-noisy-throughout-with-highamplitude-fluctuations-and-no-clear-structure-illustrating-how-curriculum-ordering-stabilizes-the-training-signal'>7<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:rewardmmeanfftvvsnnc  --></a> contrasts reward trajectories between the <span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>FT </span>model and the No Curriculum (<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>NC</span>) ablation,
showing that curriculum learning produces smoother, more stable optimization while <span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>NC </span>is noisier and
more chaotic.
</p>
<!-- l. 265 --><p class='noindent'><span class='paragraphHead' id='reward-ablations'><a id='x1-27000'></a><span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>Reward ablations</span></span>
   We implemented the multiplicative reward based on the following intuition: adding the similarity
term to the routing term could yield highly positive rewards even when the router provides
no reward. Instead, we wanted the signal to be zero whenever the routing reward is absent
or negative. In practice, however, the additive and multiplicative variants appear to behave
similarly.
</p>
   <h3 class='sectionHead' id='ethical-considerations'><span class='titlemark'>5    </span> <a id='x1-280005'></a>Ethical considerations</h3>
<!-- l. 276 --><p class='noindent'>This research studies the vulnerability of LLM routers to adversarial paraphrasing, This creates a dual-use
risk: malicious actors could exploit such attacks at scale to inflate infrastructure costs for model providers,
while understanding them is necessary for researchers and system designers to evaluate and make routing
systems more robust.
</p><!-- l. 280 --><p class='indent'>   Our paraphraser was trained only on English text from public question-answering datasets. Although
these data do not contain sensitive information, they may reflect English-centric biases. Defenses built on
such models must be evaluated carefully to avoid penalizing unconventional language, which could
disproportionately affect non-native English speakers, users with speech or cognitive disabilities, and
speakers of dialectal varieties. Signed-language interaction falls outside the scope of this work, as most
routers operate exclusively on text.
</p><!-- l. 282 --><p class='indent'>   Extending our work beyond English introduces dependencies on multilingual paraphrasers, similarity
metrics, NLI models, and routers. High-resource languages would require replacing T5 with its
multilingual variant mT5 [<a href='#XDBLP_journals_corr_abs-2010-11934'>18</a>] and validating all components through native-speaker review and
multilingual benchmarks. Low-resource languages pose greater challenges, since reliable routers and
similarity metrics may be unavailable or weak. Gathering paraphraser training data for RL training is
simpler, as our approach requires only collections of relatively simple questions available in many
lower-resource settings.
</p>
   <h3 class='sectionHead' id='conclusion'><span class='titlemark'>6    </span> <a id='x1-290006'></a>Conclusion</h3>
<!-- l. 290 --><p class='noindent'>We presented a fine-tuned paraphraser <!-- l. 290 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><msub><mrow><mi>G</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>𝜃</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>
able to perform adversarial attacks on a set of chosen LLM routers. Under the
conservative configuration (<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>FT</span>), we reach an average in-distribution ASR of
<!-- l. 290 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>20</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>0</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> at a mean semantic
similarity of <!-- l. 290 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.942</mn></mrow></math></span>.
Relaxing the similarity constraints (<span class='ptmb8t-x-x-109'>AGG</span>) raises the average ASR to
<!-- l. 290 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>63</mn><mo class='MathClass-punc' stretchy='false'>.</mo><mn>1</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span> at a similarity
of <!-- l. 290 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>0.907</mn></mrow></math></span>, at
the cost of lower meaning preservation. Our attacks generalize well on unseen queries
(Figure <a href='#outofdistribution-evaluation-on-ifeval-test-asr-of-the-two-final-models-ft-agg-against-all-six-routers-on-heldout-instructionfollowing-prompts-that-are-new-to-both-the-paraphraser-and-the-routers-the-legend-reports-each-models-mean-semantic-similarity-the-cosine-similarity-between-the-stellaenbv-embeddings-of-the-original-query-q-and-its-paraphrase-q-adv-averaged-over-the-prompts-sectionref-subsecevaluation-the-attack-generalizes-offdistribution-on-the-training-routers-chayan-for-ftagg-routellmbert-routellmcausal-reproducing-the-indistribution-ranking-at-comparable-similarity-while-the-heldout-routers-stay-essentially-unattacked-mf-sw-at-r-generalization-is-therefore-to-new-data-not-new-routers'>10<!-- tex4ht:ref: fig:oodiifeval  --></a>). Our main limitations are (i) low ASR on <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>RouteLLM-Causal </span>under tight
similarity constraints and (ii) poor transferability to unseen routers, with average ASR below
<!-- l. 290 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>1.1</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>, though <span class='t1-zi4r-0-x-x-109'>R2 </span>shows a weak
but non-zero signal (<!-- l. 290 --><span class="report-inline-math"><math display='inline' xmlns='http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML'><mrow><mn>2.7</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow></math></span>).
Our ablations show the necessity of the semantic similarity term and length penalty to preserve the
meaning of the paraphrases.
</p><!-- l. 292 --><p class='indent'>   For future work, we could: (i) fine-tune an LLM with the same reward to test whether added capacity
improves ASR without increasing semantic drift; (ii) generate a synthetic dataset of paraphrases for an
SFT warm-up before our RL pipeline.
</p><!-- l. 296 --><p class='indent'>
</p>
